The following description:

- The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.

- Systems should implement carefully considered access control policies from lower privilege modes to physical entropy sources.

- On attempted access to seed, the system can trap the access and feed a less privileged client virtual entropy source data instead of invoking an SP 800-90B or PTG.2 physical entropy source.

- Implementations may implement mseccfg such that [s,u]seed is a read-only constant value 0.

- Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.

RULE 2: Software may discover whether access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S modes by writing 1 to the mseccfg [s,u]seed fields and reading back the result.

The following description:

- The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.

- The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves. Such modules can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows a security boundary to be drawn around a component with respect to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even when the component is not in M mode.

RULE 2: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves.

RULE 3: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows trusted enclave modules to enforce the entire key lifecycle, from birth in the entropy source to death through zeroization, without passing the key across the enclave boundary to external code.

| Number | Privilege | Name      | Description                                 |
|--------|-----------|-----------|---------------------------------------------|
| 0x747  | MRW       | mseccfg   | Machine security configuration register.    |

RULE 5: The RV32-only mseccfgh CSR containing the upper 32 bits of mseccfg is located at address 0x757.
RULE 6: The RV32-only mseccfgh CSR containing the upper 32 bits of mseccfg has MRW access.

The following description:

1. mseccfg register

- mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features.
- mseccfg exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented. Otherwise, it is reserved.
- mseccfg is formatted as shown in Figure 26.

Figure 26. Machine security configuration (mseccfg) register.

<image>


2. SSEED and USEED fields

- The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.

- When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- When USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, USEED is read-only zero.

- When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, SSEED is read-only zero.

- When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

Table 20. Entropy Source Access Control.

| Mode  | SSEED | USEED | Description |
|-------|-------|-------|-------------|
| M     | -     | -     | The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal, with a CSR read-write instruction. Attempted read without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits. |
| U     | -     | 0     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| U     | -     | 1     | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write. |
| S/HS  | 0     | -     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| S/HS  | 1     | -     | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write. |
| VS/VU | 0     | -     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| VS/VU | 1     | -     | A read-write seed access raises a virtual-instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal-instruction exception. |


3. RLB, MMWP, and MML fields

- The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in mseccfg.

3.1. mseccfg.RLB

- mseccfg.RLB, Rule Locking Bypass, is a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify Locked PMP rules.

- When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.

- When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry, including disabled entries, mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.

- This feature is intended to be used as a debug mechanism, or as a temporary workaround during the boot process for simplifying software and optimizing the allocation of memory and PMP rules.

- Using this functionality under normal operation, after the boot process is completed, should be avoided since it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.

- Vendors who do not need this functionality may hardwire mseccfg.RLB to 0.

3.2. mseccfg.MMWP

- mseccfg.MMWP, Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy, is a WARL field.

- mseccfg.MMWP changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule.

- mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.

- When mseccfg.MMWP is set, it changes the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule to denied instead of ignored.

3.3. mseccfg.MML

- mseccfg.MML, Machine Mode Lockdown, is a WARL field.

- The MML bit changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit.

- mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.

When mseccfg.MML is set, the system's behavior changes as follows:

1. The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes. Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, pmpcfg.L marks a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset.

2. The formerly reserved encoding pmpcfg.RW=01 and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encode a Shared-Region.

3. An M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode.

4. An M-mode-only rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

5. An S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.

6. A Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits:

   - A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, so it is not executable. M-mode has read/write access to that region. S/U-mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.

   - A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode, so it is not writable. Both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and M-mode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

   - The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

7. Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible, and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged.

8. The restriction on adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB, for example during the boot process.

9. Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.

10. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.

- If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.


4. PMM field

- If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode according to the values in Table 21.

- If Smmpm is not implemented, PMM is read-only zero.

- The PMM field is read-only zero for RV32.

Table 21. Legal values of PMM WARL field.

| Value | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| 00 | Pointer masking is disabled, with PMLEN = 0. |
| 01 | Reserved. |
| 10 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 57, which is PMLEN = 7 on RV64. |
| 11 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 48, which is PMLEN = 16 on RV64. |

- Smmpm implementations need to satisfy:

  max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits

  to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.


5. MLPE field

- The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg.

- When the MLPE field is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.

- When the MLPE field is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode, and the following rules apply to M-mode:

  - The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.
  - The LPAD instruction operates as a no-op.


6. mseccfgh alias

- When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.

- mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented.

- mseccfgh does not exist when XLEN=64.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit register.
RULE 2: mseccfg is a read/write register.
RULE 3: mseccfg controls security features.
RULE 4: mseccfg exists if any implemented extension adds a field to mseccfg.
RULE 5: mseccfg is reserved if no implemented extension adds a field to mseccfg.
RULE 6: The Zkr extension adds the USEED field to mseccfg.
RULE 7: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED field to mseccfg.
RULE 8: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, any access to the seed CSR from U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, an access to the seed CSR from U-mode other than a read-write access raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.USEED is read-only zero.
RULE 12: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any access to the seed CSR from S-mode or HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-mode or HS-mode is allowed.
RULE 14: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, an access to the seed CSR from S-mode or HS-mode other than a read-write access raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 15: If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.SSEED is read-only zero.
RULE 16: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any access to the seed CSR from VS-mode or VU-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 17: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a read-write access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction in VS-mode or VU-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 18: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, an access to the seed CSR from VS-mode or VU-mode other than a read-write access raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 19: Regardless of mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED, the seed CSR is always available in M-mode through a CSR read-write instruction.
RULE 20: An attempted read of the seed CSR without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception in every mode regardless of mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED.
RULE 21: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB field to mseccfg.
RULE 22: The Smepmp extension adds the MMWP field to mseccfg.
RULE 23: The Smepmp extension adds the MML field to mseccfg.
RULE 24: mseccfg.RLB is the Rule Locking Bypass field and is WARL.
RULE 25: mseccfg.RLB provides a mechanism to temporarily modify locked PMP rules.
RULE 26: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed, modified, or edited.
RULE 27: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry, including a disabled entry, mseccfg.RLB remains 0 until a PMP reset.
RULE 28: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry, including a disabled entry, further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.
RULE 29: mseccfg.RLB is intended for use as a debug mechanism or as a temporary boot-process workaround for simplifying software and optimizing the allocation of memory and PMP rules.
RULE 30: Use of mseccfg.RLB during normal operation after the boot process is completed should be avoided because it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.
RULE 31: mseccfg.MMWP is the Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy field and is WARL.
RULE 32: mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit that cannot be unset after being set until a PMP reset.
RULE 33: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, the default PMP policy for M-mode access to a memory region without a matching PMP rule is denied instead of ignored.
RULE 34: mseccfg.MML is the Machine Mode Lockdown field and is WARL.
RULE 35: mseccfg.MML changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit.
RULE 36: mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit that cannot be unset after being set until a PMP reset.
RULE 37: When mseccfg.MML is set, a set pmpcfg.L bit marks a PMP rule as M-mode-only instead of marking it as locked and enforced in all modes.
RULE 38: When mseccfg.MML is set, an unset pmpcfg.L bit marks a PMP rule as S/U-mode-only.
RULE 39: When mseccfg.MML is set, the formerly reserved pmpcfg.RW=01 encoding encodes a Shared-Region.
RULE 40: When mseccfg.MML is set, the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding encodes a Shared-Region.
RULE 41: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only PMP rule is enforced on M-mode.
RULE 42: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only PMP rule denies access from S-mode and U-mode.
RULE 43: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only PMP rule remains locked so that modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 44: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only PMP rule is enforced on S-mode and U-mode.
RULE 45: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only PMP rule denies access from M-mode.
RULE 46: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule is enforced on all modes.
RULE 47: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and an unset pmpcfg.L bit can be used to share data between M-mode and S-mode or U-mode.
RULE 48: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and an unset pmpcfg.L bit is not executable.
RULE 49: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode has read/write access to a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and an unset pmpcfg.L bit.
RULE 50: When mseccfg.MML is set, S-mode and U-mode have read access to a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01, an unset pmpcfg.L bit, and an unset pmpcfg.X bit.
RULE 51: When mseccfg.MML is set, S-mode and U-mode have read/write access to a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01, an unset pmpcfg.L bit, and a set pmpcfg.X bit.
RULE 52: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and a set pmpcfg.L bit can be used to share code between M-mode and S-mode or U-mode.
RULE 53: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and a set pmpcfg.L bit is not writable.
RULE 54: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode, S-mode, and U-mode have execute access to a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and a set pmpcfg.L bit.
RULE 55: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode also has read access to a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and set pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits.
RULE 56: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.RW=01 and a set pmpcfg.L bit remains locked so that modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 57: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used to share data between M-mode and S-mode or U-mode.
RULE 58: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode, S-mode, and U-mode have only read-only access to a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.LRWX=1111.
RULE 59: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule encoded with pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 remains locked so that modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 60: When mseccfg.MML is set, adding an executable M-mode-only PMP rule or an executable locked Shared-Region PMP rule is not possible unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 61: When mseccfg.MML is set and mseccfg.RLB is not set, a pmpcfg write that attempts to add an executable M-mode-only PMP rule or an executable locked Shared-Region PMP rule is ignored and leaves pmpcfg unchanged.
RULE 62: When mseccfg.MML is set, setting mseccfg.RLB can temporarily lift the restriction on adding an executable M-mode-only PMP rule or an executable locked Shared-Region PMP rule, such as during the boot process.
RULE 63: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with M-mode privileges is possible only from a memory region with a matching M-mode-only PMP rule or a matching locked Shared-Region PMP rule with executable privileges.
RULE 64: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with M-mode privileges from a memory region without a matching PMP rule is denied.
RULE 65: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with M-mode privileges from a memory region with a matching S/U-mode-only PMP rule is denied.
RULE 66: When mseccfg.MML is not set, the pmpcfg.RW=01 encoding remains reserved for future standard use.
RULE 67: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode according to its encoded value.
RULE 68: mseccfg.PMM is a WARL field.
RULE 69: If the Smmpm extension is not implemented, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.
RULE 70: On RV32, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.
RULE 71: The mseccfg.PMM value 00 disables pointer masking and sets PMLEN to 0.
RULE 72: The mseccfg.PMM value 01 is reserved.
RULE 73: The mseccfg.PMM value 10 enables pointer masking with PMLEN equal to XLEN - 57, which is 7 on RV64.
RULE 74: The mseccfg.PMM value 11 enables pointer masking with PMLEN equal to XLEN - 48, which is 16 on RV64.
RULE 75: An Smmpm implementation of mseccfg.PMM needs to satisfy max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits to avoid masking logic on the TLB access path.
RULE 76: The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field to mseccfg.
RULE 77: When mseccfg.MLPE is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
RULE 78: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode.
RULE 79: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the hart does not update the ELP state during M-mode execution, and the ELP state remains NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 80: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op in M-mode.
RULE 81: When XLEN=32, the mseccfg alias mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register.
RULE 82: When XLEN=32, mseccfgh aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
RULE 83: mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 84: When XLEN=64, mseccfgh does not exist as an alias of mseccfg.
======================================================= END - 4 ========================================================

The following description:

1. Upon reset, if the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg.MLPE field is reset to 0.

RULE 2: Upon reset, the mseccfg.MML field is set to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

RULE 3: Upon reset, the mseccfg.MMWP field is set to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

RULE 4: Upon reset, the mseccfg.RLB field is set to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

RULE 5: Upon reset, the mseccfg.USEED field MUST have a defined reset value.

RULE 6: Upon reset, the mseccfg.SSEED field MUST have a defined reset value.

RULE 7: After reset, the system MUST NOT allow the mseccfg.USEED field to be in an undefined state.

RULE 8: After reset, the system MUST NOT allow the mseccfg.SSEED field to be in an undefined state.

The following description:

- To address the threat model outlined in Section 6.1, this extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR and their associated rules.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, the following physical memory protection rules apply:

| pmpcfg.L | pmpcfg.R | pmpcfg.W | pmpcfg.X | MMode result                                                                  | S/U Mode result                                                               |
|----------|----------|----------|----------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0        | 0        | 0        | 0        | Inaccessible region (Access Exception)                                        | Inaccessible region (Access Exception)                                        |
| 0        | 0        | 0        | 1        | Access Exception                                                              | Execute-only region                                                           |
| 0        | 0        | 1        | 0        | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode                | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode                |
| 0        | 0        | 1        | 1        | Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode                        | Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode                        |
| 0        | 1        | 0        | 0        | Access Exception                                                              | Read-only region                                                              |
| 0        | 1        | 0        | 1        | Access Exception                                                              | Read/Execute region                                                           |
| 0        | 1        | 1        | 0        | Access Exception                                                              | Read/Write region                                                             |
| 0        | 1        | 1        | 1        | Access Exception                                                              | Read/Write/Execute region                                                     |
| 1        | 0        | 0        | 0        | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception)                                | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception)                                |
| 1        | 0        | 0        | 1        | Locked Execute-only region*                                                   | Access Exception                                                              |
| 1        | 0        | 1        | 0        | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.*              | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.*              |
| 1        | 0        | 1        | 1        | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.*  | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.*  |
| 1        | 1        | 0        | 0        | Locked Read-only region*                                                      | Access Exception                                                              |
| 1        | 1        | 0        | 1        | Locked Read/Execute region*                                                   | Access Exception                                                              |
| 1        | 1        | 1        | 0        | Locked Read/Write region*                                                     | Access Exception                                                              |
| 1        | 1        | 1        | 1        | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.*                 | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.*                 |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR together with their associated rules.

RULE 2: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are all 0, the region is inaccessible in M-mode and the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 3: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are all 0, the region is inaccessible in S/U-mode and the S/U-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 4: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, and pmpcfg.W are 0, and pmpcfg.X is 1, the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, and pmpcfg.W are 0, and pmpcfg.X is 1, the region is execute-only in S/U-mode.

RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.R are 0, pmpcfg.W is 1, and pmpcfg.X is 0, the region is a shared data region with read/write access in M-mode.

RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.R are 0, pmpcfg.W is 1, and pmpcfg.X is 0, the region is a shared data region with read-only access in S/U-mode.

RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.R are 0, and pmpcfg.W and pmpcfg.X are 1, the region is a shared data region with read/write access in M-mode.

RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.R are 0, and pmpcfg.W and pmpcfg.X are 1, the region is a shared data region with read/write access in S/U-mode.

RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L is 0, pmpcfg.R is 1, and pmpcfg.W and pmpcfg.X are 0, the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L is 0, pmpcfg.R is 1, and pmpcfg.W and pmpcfg.X are 0, the region is read-only in S/U-mode.

RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.W are 0, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.X are 1, the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.W are 0, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.X are 1, the region permits read and execute access in S/U-mode.

RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X are 0, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.W are 1, the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X are 0, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.W are 1, the region permits read and write access in S/U-mode.

RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L is 0, and pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are 1, the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L is 0, and pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are 1, the region permits read, write, and execute access in S/U-mode.

RULE 18: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L is 1, and pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are 0, the region is a locked inaccessible region in M-mode and the M-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 19: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L is 1, and pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are 0, the region is a locked inaccessible region in S/U-mode and the S/U-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 20: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X are 1, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.W are 0, the region is a locked execute-only region in M-mode.

RULE 21: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X are 1, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.W are 0, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 22: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.W are 1, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.X are 0, the region is a locked shared code region with execute-only access in M-mode.

RULE 23: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.W are 1, and pmpcfg.R and pmpcfg.X are 0, the region is a locked shared code region with execute-only access in S/U-mode.

RULE 24: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are 1, and pmpcfg.R is 0, the region is a locked shared code region with read and execute access in M-mode.

RULE 25: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are 1, and pmpcfg.R is 0, the region is a locked shared code region with execute-only access in S/U-mode.

RULE 26: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.R are 1, and pmpcfg.W and pmpcfg.X are 0, the region is a locked read-only region in M-mode.

RULE 27: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.R are 1, and pmpcfg.W and pmpcfg.X are 0, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 28: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, and pmpcfg.X are 1, and pmpcfg.W is 0, the region is a locked read/execute region in M-mode.

RULE 29: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, and pmpcfg.X are 1, and pmpcfg.W is 0, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 30: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, and pmpcfg.W are 1, and pmpcfg.X is 0, the region is a locked read/write region in M-mode.

RULE 31: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, and pmpcfg.W are 1, and pmpcfg.X is 0, the S/U-mode result is an access exception.

RULE 32: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are all 1, the region is a locked shared data region with read-only access in M-mode.

RULE 33: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and pmpcfg.L, pmpcfg.R, pmpcfg.W, and pmpcfg.X are all 1, the region is a locked shared data region with read-only access in S/U-mode.

The following description:

- When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set, WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 2: When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit in hstatus is set, the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual-instruction exception.

The following description:

1. A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and shadowed in sstatus[10:9]. It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.

2. Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.

3. When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.

4. Implementations may change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

5. Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization. Software will typically use VS to reduce context-swap overhead.

6. If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

7. If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.

8. Implementations may have a writable misa.V field. Analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.

9. Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The vector context status field mstatus.VS occupies mstatus[10:9].

RULE 2: The mstatus.VS field is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].

RULE 3: The mstatus.VS field is defined analogously to mstatus.FS, the floating-point context status field.

RULE 4: When mstatus.VS is Off, an attempt to execute any vector instruction or access any vector CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 5: When mstatus.VS is Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including an instruction that changes a vector CSR, changes mstatus.VS to Dirty.

RULE 6: The mstatus.VS field may change from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when vector state has not changed.

RULE 7: Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization.

RULE 8: Software will typically use mstatus.VS to reduce context-swap overhead.

RULE 9: When mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

RULE 10: When mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.VS makes no Dirty-state contribution to mstatus.SD.

RULE 11: The mstatus.VS field may exist even when misa.V is clear.

RULE 12: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation.

RULE 13: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with a writable misa.V field.
======================================================= END - 11 =======================================================

The following description:

- When V=1, both vsstatus.VS and mstatus.VS are in effect. Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when either vsstatus.VS or mstatus.VS is set to Off.

The following description:

1. If the floating-point unit status field mstatus.FS is Off, then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

2. Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.

3. If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, the vsstatus.FS field is additionally in effect for vector floating-point instructions.

4. If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, and if vsstatus.FS or mstatus.FS is Off, then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

5. If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If mstatus.FS is Off, any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 2: Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies floating-point extension state, comprising floating-point CSRs or f registers, must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.

RULE 3: If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, vsstatus.FS is additionally in effect alongside mstatus.FS for vector floating-point instructions.

RULE 4: If the hypervisor extension is implemented, V=1, and either vsstatus.FS or mstatus.FS is Off, any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 5: If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, any vector floating-point instruction that modifies floating-point extension state, comprising floating-point CSRs or f registers, must set both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty.

The following description:

- The mstatus register is an MXLEN-bit read/write register.

- The mstatus register keeps track of and controls the hart's current operating state.

| 6      | UBE     |
| 5      | SPIE    |
| 4      | WPRI    |
| 3      | MIE     |
| 2      | WPRI    |
| 1      | SIE     |
| 0      | WPRI    |

| 6      | GVA   |
| 5      | MBE   |
| 4      | SBE   |

The following description:

1. Global interrupt-enable bits MIE and SIE are provided for M-mode and S-mode, respectively. These bits are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

2. The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

3. When a hart is executing in privilege mode x:
   - Interrupts are globally enabled when xIE=1.
   - Interrupts are globally disabled when xIE=0.

4. When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for lower-privilege modes w, where w < x, are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global wIE bit for the lower-privilege mode.

5. When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for higher-privilege modes y, where y > x, are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global yIE bit for the higher-privilege mode.

6. Higher-privilege-level code can use separate per-interrupt enable bits to disable selected higher-privilege-mode interrupts before ceding control to a lower-privilege mode.

7. If supervisor mode is not implemented, SIE and SPIE are read-only 0.

8. To support nested traps, each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts has a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes:
   - xPIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap.
   - xPP holds the previous privilege mode.

9. The xPP fields can only hold privilege modes up to x. MPP is two bits wide, and SPP is one bit wide.

10. When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x:
    - xPIE is set to the value of xIE.
    - xIE is set to 0.
    - xPP is set to y.

11. For lower privilege modes, any trap, synchronous or asynchronous, is usually taken at a higher privilege mode with interrupts disabled upon entry.

12. The higher-level trap handler will either service the trap and return using the stacked information or, if not returning immediately to the interrupted context, will save the privilege stack before re-enabling interrupts, so only one entry per stack is required.

13. An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode, respectively.

14. When executing an xRET instruction, supposing xPP holds the value y:
    - xIE is set to xPIE.
    - The privilege mode is changed to y.
    - xPIE is set to 1.
    - xPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode: U if U-mode is implemented, else M.

15. When executing an xRET instruction, if y != M, xRET also sets MPRV=0.

16. Setting xPP to the least-privileged supported mode on an xRET helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

17. Trap handlers must be designed to neither enable interrupts nor cause exceptions during the phase of handling where the trap handler preserves the critical state information required to handle and resume from the trap. An exception or interrupt in this critical phase of trap handling may lead to a trap that can overwrite such critical state. This could result in the loss of data needed to recover from the initial trap.

18. If an exception occurs in the code path needed to handle traps, such a situation may lead to an infinite loop of traps. To prevent this, trap handlers must be meticulously designed to identify and safely manage exceptions within their operational flow.

19. xPP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.

20. If privilege mode x is not implemented, xPP must be read-only 0.

21. M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to MPP and then reading it back.

22. If the machine provides only U and M modes, only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in MPP.
============================================================

RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MIE bit in mstatus is the global interrupt-enable bit for M-mode.

RULE 2: The SIE bit in mstatus is the global interrupt-enable bit for S-mode.

RULE 3: The MIE bit in mstatus is primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

RULE 4: The SIE bit in mstatus is primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

RULE 5: The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

RULE 6: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled if the xIE bit in mstatus is 1.

RULE 7: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally disabled if the xIE bit in mstatus is 0.

RULE 8: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts for every lower privilege mode w where w < x are always globally disabled regardless of the corresponding global wIE bit in mstatus.

RULE 9: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts for every higher privilege mode y where y > x are always globally enabled regardless of the corresponding global yIE bit in mstatus.

RULE 10: If supervisor mode is not implemented, the SIE field in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 11: If supervisor mode is not implemented, the SPIE field in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 12: To support nested traps, mstatus provides a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes for each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts.

RULE 13: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, the xPIE field in mstatus holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit that was active before the trap.

RULE 14: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, the xPP field in mstatus holds the previous privilege mode.

RULE 15: Each xPP field in mstatus is a WARL field that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.

RULE 16: The MPP field in mstatus is two bits wide.

RULE 17: The SPP field in mstatus is one bit wide.

RULE 18: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to the value of the xIE field in mstatus.

RULE 19: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xIE field in mstatus is set to 0.

RULE 20: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPP field in mstatus is set to y.

RULE 21: Only one saved entry is required in each mstatus privilege stack because a higher-level trap handler will either service the trap and return using the stacked information or save the privilege stack before re-enabling interrupts when it will not return immediately to the interrupted context.

RULE 22: An MRET instruction is used to apply the M-mode trap-return behavior associated with mstatus.

RULE 23: An SRET instruction is used to apply the S-mode trap-return behavior associated with mstatus.

RULE 24: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the xIE field in mstatus is set to the value of the xPIE field in mstatus.

RULE 25: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the privilege mode is changed to y.

RULE 26: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to 1.

RULE 27: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the xPP field is set to the least-privileged supported mode, which is U if U-mode is implemented and M otherwise.

RULE 28: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding a privilege mode y where y != M, the MPRV field in mstatus is also set to 0.

RULE 29: Setting the xPP field in mstatus to the least-privileged supported mode on an xRET instruction helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

RULE 30: If privilege mode x is not implemented, the corresponding xPP field in mstatus must be read-only 0.

RULE 31: M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that privilege mode to the MPP field in mstatus and then reading MPP back.

RULE 32: If a machine provides only U-mode and M-mode, only one hardware storage bit is required for the MPP field in mstatus to represent either 00 or 11.

The following description:

- The M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension.

- Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1.

- When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0.

- For RV64, when the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE bit is cleared to 0 regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.

- The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write.

- For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit is in mstatus.

- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit is currently 0, it is set to 1 and the trap is delivered as expected.

- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit is already set to 1, the trap is an unexpected trap.

- When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.

- A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit.

- A trap that occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 is an unexpected trap.

- In the event of an unexpected trap, when the Smrnmi extension is implemented and mnstatus.NMIE is 1, the hart traps to the RNMI handler.

- In the event of an unexpected trap, when the Smrnmi extension is not implemented, or if the Smrnmi extension is implemented and mnstatus.NMIE is 0, the hart enters a critical-error state without updating any architectural state, including the pc. This state involves ceasing execution, disabling all interrupts, including NMIs, and asserting a critical-error signal to the platform.

- The MRET and SRET instructions, when executed in M-mode, set the MDT bit to 0.

- The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.

RULE 2: The M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) field represented in mstatus or mstatush is introduced by the Smdbltrp extension.

RULE 3: Upon reset, the MDT field represented in mstatus or mstatush is set to 1.

RULE 4: When an explicit CSR write sets the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush to 1, the MIE bit in mstatus is cleared to 0.

RULE 5: On RV64, when an explicit CSR write sets mstatus.MDT to 1, mstatus.MIE is cleared to 0 regardless of the value written, if any, to mstatus.MIE by the same write.

RULE 6: An explicit CSR write can set mstatus.MIE to 1 only if the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush is already 0 or, on RV64, the same write sets mstatus.MDT to 0.

RULE 7: On RV32, the MDT field associated with mstatus is located in mstatush.

RULE 8: On RV32, the MIE bit is located in mstatus.

RULE 9: When a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush is currently 0, the MDT bit is set to 1.

RULE 10: When a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush is currently 0, the trap is delivered as expected.

RULE 11: When a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush is already 1, the trap is an unexpected trap.

RULE 12: When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush.

RULE 13: A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush.

RULE 16: In the mstatus MDT handling model, when an unexpected trap occurs and either the Smrnmi extension is not implemented or the Smrnmi extension is implemented with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0, the hart enters a critical-error state.

RULE 17: In the mstatus MDT handling model, when an unexpected trap occurs and either the Smrnmi extension is not implemented or the Smrnmi extension is implemented with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0, the hart does not update any architectural state, including the pc.

RULE 18: In the mstatus MDT handling model, when an unexpected trap occurs and either the Smrnmi extension is not implemented or the Smrnmi extension is implemented with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0, the hart ceases execution in the critical-error state.

RULE 19: In the mstatus MDT handling model, when an unexpected trap occurs and either the Smrnmi extension is not implemented or the Smrnmi extension is implemented with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0, the hart disables all interrupts, including NMIs, in the critical-error state.

RULE 20: In the mstatus MDT handling model, when an unexpected trap occurs and either the Smrnmi extension is not implemented or the Smrnmi extension is implemented with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0, the hart asserts a critical-error signal to the platform.

RULE 21: When MRET is executed in M-mode, it sets the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush to 0.

RULE 22: When SRET is executed in M-mode, it sets the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush to 0.

RULE 23: When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, MNRET sets the MDT bit in mstatus or mstatush to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.

The following description:

- For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields in mstatus are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and U-mode, respectively. The encoding of these fields is the same as the MXL field of misa. The effective XLEN in S-mode and U-mode are termed SXLEN and UXLEN, respectively.

- When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.

- When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is not supported, then SXL is read-only zero.

- When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is supported, SXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN.

- When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.

- When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is not supported, then UXL is read-only zero.

- When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is supported, UXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN.

- When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.

- If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

- Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN and must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.

- Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, pc bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the pc is written, it is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

- Operations always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.

- The architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode. Machine operation is well-defined even when lower-privilege modes do not have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode.

- When a HINT encoded as an integer computational instruction that overwrites its destination register with its current value, such as `c.addi x8, 0`, is executed with XLEN < MXLEN and bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

- This HINT behavior allows implementations to elide register write-back for some HINTs while allowing them to execute other HINTs in the same manner as other integer computational instructions. The implementation choice is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: For an RV64 hart, the SXL field of mstatus is a WARL field.

RULE 2: For an RV64 hart, the SXL field of mstatus controls the XLEN value for S-mode.

RULE 3: For an RV64 hart, the UXL field of mstatus is a WARL field.

RULE 4: For an RV64 hart, the UXL field of mstatus controls the XLEN value for U-mode.

RULE 5: For an RV64 hart, the encoding of the SXL field of mstatus is the same as the encoding of the MXL field of misa.

RULE 6: For an RV64 hart, the encoding of the UXL field of mstatus is the same as the encoding of the MXL field of misa.

RULE 7: For an RV64 hart, the effective S-mode XLEN controlled by the SXL field of mstatus is termed SXLEN.

RULE 8: For an RV64 hart, the effective U-mode XLEN controlled by the UXL field of mstatus is termed UXLEN.

RULE 9: When MXLEN=32, the SXL field does not exist in mstatus.

RULE 10: When MXLEN=32, SXLEN=32 even though the SXL field does not exist in mstatus.

RULE 11: When MXLEN=32, the UXL field does not exist in mstatus.

RULE 12: When MXLEN=32, UXLEN=32 even though the UXL field does not exist in mstatus.

RULE 13: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is not supported, the SXL field of mstatus is read-only zero.

RULE 14: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is supported, the SXL field of mstatus is a WARL field.

RULE 15: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is supported, the SXL field of mstatus encodes the current value of SXLEN.

RULE 16: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is not supported, the UXL field of mstatus is read-only zero.

RULE 17: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is supported, the UXL field of mstatus is a WARL field.

RULE 18: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is supported, the UXL field of mstatus encodes the current value of UXLEN.

RULE 19: If S-mode is implemented, the legal values of the UXL field of mstatus exclude values that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

RULE 20: Whenever an mstatus field sets XLEN for a mode to less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN.

RULE 21: Whenever an mstatus field sets XLEN for a mode to less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.

RULE 22: Whenever an mstatus field sets XLEN for a mode to less than the widest supported XLEN, pc bits above the configured XLEN are ignored.

RULE 23: Whenever the pc is written while an mstatus field configures XLEN for a mode to less than the widest supported XLEN, the pc is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

RULE 24: Regardless of the XLEN values configured by mstatus, operations always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.

RULE 25: The mstatus XLEN configuration is not subject to an architectural check requiring a lower-privilege mode's XLEN setting to be less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode's XLEN setting.

RULE 26: Machine operation is well-defined even when the mstatus XLEN configuration gives a lower-privilege mode an XLEN setting that is not less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode's XLEN setting.

RULE 27: When a HINT encoded as an integer computational instruction that overwrites its destination register with its current value, such as c.addi x8, 0, executes under an mstatus-configured XLEN with XLEN < MXLEN and bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether those bits remain unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

RULE 28: When a HINT encoded as an integer computational instruction overwrites its destination register with its current value under an mstatus-configured XLEN with XLEN < MXLEN while bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN-1, the choice between leaving those bits unchanged and overwriting them with copies of bit XLEN-1 is observable only by privilege modes whose XLEN setting is greater than the current XLEN.

RULE 29: When a HINT encoded as an integer computational instruction overwrites its destination register with its current value under an mstatus-configured XLEN with XLEN < MXLEN while bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN-1, the choice between leaving those bits unchanged and overwriting them with copies of bit XLEN-1 is invisible to the current privilege mode.

The following description:

1. MPRV (Modify PRiVilege)

- The MPRV bit modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.

- When MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.

- When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.

- Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV.

- MPRV is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.

- An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV=0.

- The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by MPP might have different XLEN settings. When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.

- While SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode, SUM is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S.

- The MPRV mechanism was conceived to improve the efficiency of M-mode routines that emulate missing hardware features, e.g., misaligned loads and stores. MPRV obviates the need to perform address translation in software.

2. MXR (Make eXecutable Readable)

- The MXR bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.

- When MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1) will succeed.

- When MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed.

- MXR has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.

- MXR is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.

- The MXR mechanism was conceived to improve the efficiency of M-mode routines that emulate missing hardware features, e.g., misaligned loads and stores. MXR allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.

- The MXR mechanism only affects the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries. In particular, MXR has no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

3. SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access)

- The SUM bit modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.

- When SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1) will fault.

- When SUM=1, these accesses are permitted.

- SUM has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.

- While SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode, SUM is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S.

- SUM is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read-only 0.

- The SUM mechanism only affects the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries. In particular, SUM has no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.
============================================================

RULE 1: The mstatus.MPRV bit modifies the effective privilege mode at which explicit memory accesses execute.

RULE 2: When mstatus.MPRV=0, address translation for explicit memory accesses uses the translation mechanisms of the current privilege mode.

RULE 3: When mstatus.MPRV=0, protection for explicit memory accesses uses the protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.

RULE 4: When mstatus.MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated as though the current privilege mode were the privilege mode encoded in mstatus.MPP.

RULE 5: When mstatus.MPRV=1, loads and stores are protected as though the current privilege mode were the privilege mode encoded in mstatus.MPP.

RULE 6: When mstatus.MPRV=1, endianness is applied to loads and stores as though the current privilege mode were the privilege mode encoded in mstatus.MPP.

RULE 7: Instruction address translation is unaffected by the setting of mstatus.MPRV.

RULE 8: Instruction protection is unaffected by the setting of mstatus.MPRV.

RULE 9: The mstatus.MPRV bit is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.

RULE 10: An MRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M-mode also sets mstatus.MPRV=0.

RULE 11: An SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M-mode also sets mstatus.MPRV=0.

RULE 12: The current privilege mode and the privilege mode encoded in mstatus.MPP can have different XLEN settings.

RULE 13: When mstatus.MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were the XLEN of the privilege mode encoded in mstatus.MPP.

RULE 14: The mstatus.SUM bit is ordinarily ignored when execution is not in S-mode.

RULE 15: The mstatus.SUM bit is in effect when mstatus.MPRV=1 and mstatus.MPP=S.

RULE 16: The mstatus.MXR bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.

RULE 17: When page-based virtual memory is in effect and mstatus.MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable with R=1 succeed with respect to page-table permissions.

RULE 18: When page-based virtual memory is in effect and mstatus.MXR=1, loads from pages marked readable or executable with R=1 or X=1 succeed with respect to page-table permissions.

RULE 19: The mstatus.MXR bit has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.

RULE 20: The mstatus.MXR bit is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.

RULE 21: The mstatus.MXR mechanism allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.

RULE 22: The mstatus.MXR mechanism affects only the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.

RULE 23: The mstatus.MXR mechanism has no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

RULE 24: The mstatus.SUM bit modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.

RULE 25: When page-based virtual memory is in effect and mstatus.SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages accessible by U-mode with U=1 fault.

RULE 26: When page-based virtual memory is in effect and mstatus.SUM=1, S-mode memory accesses to pages accessible by U-mode with U=1 are permitted.

RULE 27: The mstatus.SUM bit has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.

RULE 28: The mstatus.SUM bit is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.

RULE 29: The mstatus.SUM bit is read-only 0 if satp.MODE is read-only 0.

RULE 30: The mstatus.SUM mechanism affects only the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.

RULE 31: The mstatus.SUM mechanism has no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

The following description:

- The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches. Instruction fetches are always little-endian.

- For non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode, assuming mstatus.MPRV=0:
  - MBE=0 selects little-endian accesses.
  - MBE=1 selects big-endian accesses.

- If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0.

- Otherwise, for explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode:
  - SBE=0 selects little-endian accesses.
  - SBE=1 selects big-endian accesses.

- If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0.

- Otherwise, for explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode:
  - UBE=0 selects little-endian accesses.
  - UBE=1 selects big-endian accesses.

- For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by SBE.

- Since changing SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of supervisor-level memory management data structures, if any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0.

- Only in contrived scenarios will a given memory-management data structure be interpreted as both little-endian and big-endian.

- In practice, SBE will only be changed at runtime on world switches, in which case neither the old nor new memory-management data structure will be reinterpreted in a different endianness. In this case, no additional SFENCE.VMA is necessary beyond what would ordinarily be required for a world switch.

- If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.

- If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.

- If MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0, the implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses.

- If MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported, the implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses aside from instruction fetches.

- A hart's address space is a circular sequence of 2^XLEN bytes at consecutive addresses. The correspondence between addresses and byte locations is fixed and is not affected by any endianness mode. The applicable endianness mode instead determines the order of mapping between memory bytes and a multibyte quantity, such as a halfword or word.

- RISC-V instructions are uniformly little-endian, independently of the current endianness settings.

- In big-endian mode, software that encodes or decodes machine instructions must account for explicit loads and stores having endianness opposite that of instructions, for example by swapping byte order after loads and before stores.

RULE 16: In practice, the SBE field in mstatus or mstatush is changed at runtime only on world switches.

The following description:

TVM (Trap Virtual Memory):

- The TVM bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations.

- When TVM=1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=1, attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=1, attempts to execute a SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=0, reading or writing the satp CSR and executing SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA are permitted in S-mode.

- When S-mode is not supported, TVM is read-only 0.

- The TVM mechanism permits guest operating systems to execute in S-mode, rather than classically virtualizing them in U-mode. This approach obviates the need to trap accesses to most S-mode CSRs.

- Trapping satp accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables.

TW (Timeout Wait):

- The TW bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the WFI instruction.

- When TW=0, the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.

- When TW=1, if WFI is executed in any less-privileged mode and it does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit, the WFI instruction causes an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TW=1, an implementation may have WFI always raise an illegal-instruction exception in modes less privileged than M, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.

- When there are no modes less privileged than M, TW is read-only 0.

- When S-mode is implemented, executing WFI in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception, regardless of the value of the TW bit, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.

- Trapping the WFI instruction can trigger a world switch to another guest OS, rather than wastefully idling in the current guest.

TSR (Trap SRET):

- The TSR bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the supervisor exception return instruction, SRET.

- When TSR=1, attempts to execute SRET while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TSR=0, executing SRET is permitted in S-mode.

- When S-mode is not supported, TSR is read-only 0.

- Trapping SRET is necessary to emulate the hypervisor extension on implementations that do not provide it.
============================================================
