The following description:

- In the mstatus register, FS[1:0] and VS[1:0] are WARL fields, and XS[1:0] is a read-only field.

- FS, VS, and XS are used to reduce the cost of context save and restore by setting and tracking the current state of the floating-point unit and other user-mode extensions. A context-switch routine can check these fields to quickly determine whether a state save or restore is required. If a save or restore is required, additional instructions and CSRs are typically required to effect and optimize the process.

- The FS field encodes the status of the floating-point unit state, including floating-point registers f0-f31 and CSRs fcsr, frm, and fflags.

- The VS field encodes the status of the vector extension state, including vector registers v0-v31 and CSRs vcsr, vxrm, vxsat, vstart, vl, vtype, and vlenb.

- The XS field encodes the status of additional user-mode extensions and associated state.

- To date, the V extension is the only standard extension that defines additional state beyond the floating-point CSRs and data registers.

- The design anticipates that most context switches will not need to save or restore state in either or both of the floating-point unit or other extensions, and provides a fast check through the SD bit.

- FS, VS, and XS use the following status encoding:

| Status | FS and VS meaning | XS meaning                   |
|--------|-------------------|------------------------------|
| 0      | Off               | All off                      |
| 1      | Initial           | None dirty or clean, some on |
| 2      | Clean             | None dirty, some clean       |
| 3      | Dirty             | Some dirty                   |

- If the F extension is implemented, the FS field shall not be read-only zero.

- If neither the F extension nor S-mode is implemented, FS is read-only zero.

- If S-mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS may optionally be read-only zero. Implementations with S-mode but without the F extension are permitted, but not required, to make FS read-only zero. Some such implementations may choose not to make FS read-only zero, so as to enable emulation of the F extension for both S-mode and U-mode through invisible traps into M-mode.

- If the v registers are implemented, the VS field shall not be read-only zero.

- If neither the v registers nor S-mode is implemented, VS is read-only zero.

- If S-mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS may optionally be read-only zero.

- In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, XS is read-only zero.

- Every additional extension with state provides a CSR field that encodes the equivalent of the XS states.

- XS represents a summary of all extensions' status. XS effectively reports the maximum status value across all user-extension status fields, although individual extensions can use a different encoding than XS.

- SD is a read-only bit that summarizes whether FS, VS, or XS signals the presence of dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory.

- SD is set when either FS, VS, or XS encodes Dirty:

  SD = (FS == 0b11 OR XS == 0b11 OR VS == 0b11)

- If FS, XS, and VS are all read-only zero, SD is also always zero.

- SD allows privileged code to quickly determine when no additional context save is required beyond the integer register set and pc.

- When an extension's status is Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state causes an illegal-instruction exception.

- When an extension's status is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.

- When an extension's status is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.

- When an extension's status is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.

- During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean.

- During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean. The status should never be Dirty at restore.

- If the status is Initial during context restore, the context must be set to an initial constant value to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory. For example, the floating-point registers can all be initialized to the immediate value 0.

- FS and XS are read by privileged code before saving the context.

- FS is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context.

- XS is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions.

- The status fields are also updated during instruction execution, regardless of privilege mode.

- A user-mode extension can define additional instructions that allow user-mode software to return the unit to an initial state or to turn off the unit.

- If an unconfigured coprocessor state is represented as Initial for context save, and the same application remains running between an unconfigure and the next configure, the state need not actually be reinitialized by the unconfigure instruction. Initial may cause the coprocessor state to be initialized to a constant value only at context restore, not at every unconfigure. The next configure would set the status to Dirty.

- Executing a user-mode instruction to disable a unit and place it into Off causes an illegal-instruction exception if any subsequent instruction tries to use the unit before it is turned back on.

- A user-mode instruction to turn a unit on must also ensure the unit's state is properly initialized, because the unit might have been used by another context in the meantime.

- Changing FS has no effect on the contents of the floating-point register state.

- Setting FS=Off does not destroy the floating-point register state.

- Setting FS=Initial does not clear the contents of the floating-point register state.

- Changing VS has no effect on the contents of the vector register state.

- Other extensions might not preserve state when set to Off.

- Implementations may track floating-point register-state dirtiness imprecisely by reporting the state as Dirty even when it has not been modified.

- On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate floating-point state may cause FS to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty.

- On implementations where floating-point dirtiness is not tracked at all, the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes FS to be set to Dirty.

- The definition of FS does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation.

- Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to close a potential side channel.

- If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating-point register or fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.

- Implementations may track vector register-state dirtiness in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean.

- When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.

- The following are all possible FS, VS, or XS state transitions:

1. At context save in privileged code:
   - If the current state is Off, do not save state; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Initial, do not save state; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Clean, do not save state; the next state is Clean.
   - If the current state is Dirty, save state; the next state is Clean.

2. At context restore in privileged code:
   - If the current state is Off, do not restore state; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Initial, restore to initial; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Clean, restore from memory; the next state is Clean.
   - If the current state is Dirty, restore state and next state are N/A.

3. Execute an instruction to read state:
   - If the current state is Off, the action is Exception; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute; the next state is Clean.
   - If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute; the next state is Dirty.

4. Execute an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration:
   - If the current state is Off, the action is Exception; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute; the next state is Dirty.
   - If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute; the next state is Dirty.
   - If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute; the next state is Dirty.

5. Execute an instruction to unconfigure the unit:
   - If the current state is Off, the action is Exception; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.

6. Execute an instruction to disable the unit:
   - If the current state is Off, the action is Execute; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute; the next state is Off.
   - If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute; the next state is Off.

7. Execute an instruction to enable the unit:
   - If the current state is Off, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.
   - If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute; the next state is Initial.

- The standard floating-point and vector extensions do not support user-mode unconfigure or disable/enable instructions.

- Standard privileged instructions to initialize, save, and restore extension state are provided to insulate privileged code from details of the added extension state by treating the state as an opaque object.

- XS provides a summary of all added extension state, but additional microarchitectural bits might be maintained in an extension to further reduce context-save and restore overhead.

- Floating-point unit state is always initialized, saved, and restored using standard F, D, and/or Q instructions. Privileged code must be aware of FLEN to determine the appropriate space to reserve for each f register.

- Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of FS, VS, and XS.

- Supervisor-level software normally uses FS, VS, and XS directly to record status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context.

- Machine-level software must be more conservative when saving and restoring extension state in its corresponding version of the context.

The following description:

1. An interrupt i will trap to M-mode, causing the privilege mode to change to M-mode, if all of the following are true:

   (a) Either the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode;

   (b) Bit i is set in both mip and mie; and

   (c) If register mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

2. These conditions for an interrupt trap to occur must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt becomes, or ceases to be, pending in mip.

3. These conditions for an interrupt trap to occur must also be evaluated immediately following the execution of an xRET instruction or an explicit write to a CSR on which these interrupt trap conditions expressly depend, including mip, mie, mstatus, and mideleg.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Interrupt i will trap to M-mode and cause the privilege mode to change to M-mode if all of the following are true: either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode; bit i is set in both mip and mie; and, if mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

RULE 2: The conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode, including the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, MUST be evaluated within a bounded amount of time from when interrupt i becomes pending in mip.

RULE 3: The conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode, including the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, MUST be evaluated within a bounded amount of time from when interrupt i ceases to be pending in mip.

RULE 4: The conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode, including the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, MUST be evaluated immediately following the execution of an xRET instruction.

RULE 5: The conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode, including the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, MUST be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to any CSR on which the interrupt-trap conditions expressly depend.

RULE 6: Because the conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode expressly depend on mip and include the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, the conditions MUST be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to mip.

RULE 7: Because the conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode expressly depend on mie and include the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, the conditions MUST be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to mie.

RULE 8: Because the conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode expressly depend on mstatus through the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, the conditions MUST be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to mstatus.

RULE 9: Because the conditions for interrupt i to trap to M-mode expressly depend on mideleg when mideleg exists and include the condition that either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, the conditions MUST be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to mideleg.

The following description:

- The WFI instruction may raise an illegal-instruction exception when TW=1 in mstatus.

- The WFI instruction can be executed when interrupts are disabled.

- The operation of WFI must be unaffected by the global interrupt bits in mstatus (MIE and SIE) and the delegation register mideleg; i.e., the hart must resume if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending, even if it has been delegated to a less-privileged mode.

- The operation of WFI should honor the individual interrupt enables (e.g., MTIE); i.e., implementations should avoid resuming the hart if the interrupt is pending but not individually enabled.

- WFI is required to resume execution for locally enabled interrupts pending at any privilege level, regardless of the global interrupt enable at each privilege level.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The WFI instruction may raise an illegal-instruction exception when the TW field in mstatus equals 1.

RULE 2: The WFI instruction can be executed when interrupts are globally disabled through mstatus.

RULE 3: The operation of WFI must be unaffected by the MIE and SIE global interrupt bits in mstatus and by the mideleg delegation register.

RULE 4: A hart executing WFI must resume when a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending, even if the global interrupt bits in mstatus disable interrupts and mideleg delegates the interrupt to a less-privileged mode.

RULE 5: WFI should honor individual interrupt enables such as MTIE by avoiding resumption when an interrupt is pending but not individually enabled, regardless of the global interrupt-bit state in mstatus.

RULE 6: WFI is required to resume execution for a locally enabled interrupt pending at any privilege level, regardless of the global interrupt-enable state in mstatus at each privilege level.

RULE 3: When mnstatus.NMIE=0, the hart behaves as though mstatus.MPRV were clear, regardless of the current setting of mstatus.MPRV.

The following description:

- When mstatus.TVM=1, an attempt to execute SINVAL.VMA or HINVAL.GVMA in S-mode or HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR are unaffected by the mstatus.TVM field and hence are always permitted in S-mode.

- When mstatus.TVM=1, SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR do not need to be trapped, as they only have ordering effects but no visible side effects. Trapping of the SINVAL.VMA instruction is sufficient to enable emulation of the intended overall TLB maintenance functionality.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When mstatus.TVM=1, an attempt to execute SINVAL.VMA in S-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: When mstatus.TVM=1, an attempt to execute SINVAL.VMA in HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: When mstatus.TVM=1, an attempt to execute HINVAL.GVMA in S-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: When mstatus.TVM=1, an attempt to execute HINVAL.GVMA in HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: The mstatus.TVM field does not affect SFENCE.W.INVAL.
RULE 6: SFENCE.W.INVAL is always permitted in S-mode regardless of the value of mstatus.TVM.
RULE 7: The mstatus.TVM field does not affect SFENCE.INVAL.IR.
RULE 8: SFENCE.INVAL.IR is always permitted in S-mode regardless of the value of mstatus.TVM.
RULE 9: When mstatus.TVM=1, SFENCE.W.INVAL does not need to be trapped because it has only ordering effects and no visible side effects.
RULE 10: When mstatus.TVM=1, SFENCE.INVAL.IR does not need to be trapped because it has only ordering effects and no visible side effects.
RULE 11: When mstatus.TVM=1, trapping SINVAL.VMA is sufficient to enable emulation of the intended overall TLB maintenance functionality.

The following description:

- The hstatus register provides facilities analogous to the mstatus register for tracking and controlling the exception behavior of a VS-mode guest.

- The hstatus fields VTSR, VTW, and VTVM are defined analogously to the mstatus fields TSR, TW, and TVM, but affect execution only in VS-mode and cause virtual-instruction exceptions instead of illegal-instruction exceptions.

- When hstatus.VTW=1 and assuming mstatus.TW=0, an attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual-instruction exception if the WFI does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.

- An implementation may have WFI always raise a virtual-instruction exception in VS-mode when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register has facilities for tracking and controlling exception behavior that are analogous to the facilities provided by hstatus for a VS-mode guest.

RULE 2: The mstatus.TSR field is defined analogously to hstatus.VTSR and is associated with illegal-instruction exceptions rather than the virtual-instruction exceptions caused by hstatus.VTSR in VS-mode.

RULE 3: The mstatus.TW field is defined analogously to hstatus.VTW and is associated with illegal-instruction exceptions rather than the virtual-instruction exceptions caused by hstatus.VTW in VS-mode.

RULE 4: The mstatus.TVM field is defined analogously to hstatus.VTVM and is associated with illegal-instruction exceptions rather than the virtual-instruction exceptions caused by hstatus.VTVM in VS-mode.

RULE 5: When mstatus.TW=0 and hstatus.VTW=1, an attempt in VS-mode to execute WFI raises a virtual-instruction exception if WFI does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.
======================================================= END - 38 =======================================================

The following description:

- The hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR and modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.

- When the hypervisor extension is implemented and MXLEN=64, MPV and GVA are fields of the modified mstatus register.

- When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA to mstatush, not to mstatus. The format of mstatus is unchanged for RV32.

- The MPV bit is Machine Previous Virtualization Mode. Whenever a trap is taken into M-mode, MPV is written by the implementation and is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap. Just as MPV records V, the MPP field is set to the nominal privilege mode at the time of the trap.

- When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.

- The GVA field is Guest Virtual Address. Whenever a trap is taken into M-mode, GVA is written by the implementation.

- For any breakpoint, address-misaligned, access-fault, page-fault, or guest-page-fault trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, GVA is set to 1.

- For any other trap into M-mode, GVA is set to 0.

- The TSR and TVM fields of mstatus affect execution only in HS-mode, not in VS-mode.

- The TW field of mstatus affects execution in all modes except M-mode.

- Setting TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.

- Setting TVM=1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.

- TVM exists in mstatus to allow machine-level software to modify the address translations managed by a supervisor-level OS, usually for the purpose of inserting another stage of address translation below that controlled by the OS.

- The instruction traps enabled by TVM=1 permit machine level to co-opt both satp and hgatp and substitute shadow page tables that merge the OS's chosen page translations with M-level's lower-stage translations, all without the OS being aware.

- M-level software needs this ability not only to emulate the hypervisor extension if not already supported, but also to emulate any future RISC-V extensions that may modify or add address translation stages, perhaps to improve support for nested hypervisors.

- Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp, instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or any actions taken in VS-mode, because M-level software is not expected to need to involve itself in VS-stage address translation.

- For virtual machines, it should be sufficient, and in all likelihood faster as well, to leave VS-stage address translation alone and merge all other translation stages into G-stage shadow page tables controlled by hgatp. This assumption places some constraints on possible future RISC-V extensions that current machines will be able to emulate efficiently.

- The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.

- When MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal.

- When MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.

- When MPRV=0, regardless of MPV and MPP, the effect is normal access; the current privilege mode applies.

- When MPRV=1, MPV=0, and MPP=0, the effect is U-level access with HS-level translation and protection only.

- When MPRV=1, MPV=0, and MPP=1, the effect is HS-level access with HS-level translation and protection only.

- When MPRV=1 and MPP=3, regardless of MPV, the effect is M-level access with no translation.

- When MPRV=1, MPV=1, and MPP=0, the effect is VU-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-level MXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage.

- When MPRV=1, MPV=1, and MPP=1, the effect is VS-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-level MXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS-level SUM bit.

- MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV.

- The explicit loads and stores of HLV, HLVX, and HSV always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding MPRV.

- The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 9: Whenever a trap is taken into M-mode, mstatus.MPP is set to the nominal privilege mode at the time of the trap.

RULE 17: For a guest-page-fault trap that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, the GVA field in mstatus when MXLEN=64 or in mstatush when MXLEN=32 is set to 1.

RULE 18: For any other trap into M-mode, the GVA field in mstatus when MXLEN=64 or in mstatush when MXLEN=32 is set to 0.

RULE 19: The mstatus.TSR field affects execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.

RULE 20: The mstatus.TVM field affects execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.

RULE 23: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from executing HFENCE.GVMA.

RULE 24: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from executing HINVAL.GVMA.

RULE 47: When mstatus.MPRV=1, MPV=0, and mstatus.MPP=1, translation and protection are HS-level only.

The following description:

1. When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR. An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode, for some values of the instruction's register operands, assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.

2. In VS-mode or VU-mode, attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR raise a virtual-instruction exception when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

3. In VS-mode or VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR raise a virtual-instruction exception when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

4. In VU-mode, attempts to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0 raise a virtual-instruction exception.

5. In VU-mode, attempts to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR raise a virtual-instruction exception when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

6. In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR raise a virtual-instruction exception when the same access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

7. In VS-mode, attempts to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0 raise a virtual-instruction exception, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.

8. TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode). They are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, an attempted instruction that would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of its register operands assuming mstatus.TSR=0 and mstatus.TVM=0 is HS-qualified and normally raises a virtual-instruction exception with code 22 instead of an illegal-instruction exception if insufficient privilege or an express disable by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR prevents the instruction from executing while V=1.

RULE 2: In VS-mode or VU-mode, an attempt to read or write an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR raises a virtual-instruction exception when the same read or write would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

RULE 3: In VS-mode or VU-mode when XLEN=32, an attempt to read or write an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR raises a virtual-instruction exception when the same read or write of the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

RULE 4: In VU-mode, an attempt to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0 raises a virtual-instruction exception.

RULE 5: In VU-mode, an attempt to read or write an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR raises a virtual-instruction exception when the same read or write would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

RULE 6: In VU-mode when XLEN=32, an attempt to read or write an implemented high-half supervisor CSR raises a virtual-instruction exception when the same read or write of the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

RULE 7: In VS-mode, an attempt to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0 raises a virtual-instruction exception unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.

RULE 8: The mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM fields are intended to affect only S-mode, which is HS-mode when the hypervisor extension is active.

RULE 9: The mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM fields are ignored when determining exceptions in VS-mode.

The following description:

- When a trap occurs in HS-mode or U-mode, it goes to M-mode unless delegated by medeleg or mideleg, in which case it goes to HS-mode.

- When a trap occurs in VS-mode or VU-mode, it goes to M-mode unless delegated by medeleg or mideleg.

- When a trap is taken into M-mode, virtualization mode V gets set to 0.

- When a trap is taken into M-mode, fields MPV and MPP in mstatus (or mstatush) are set as follows:

| Previous Mode | MPV | MPP |
|---------------|-----|-----|
| U-mode        | 0   | 0   |
| HS-mode       | 0   | 1   |
| M-mode        | 0   | 3   |
| VU-mode       | 1   | 0   |
| VS-mode       | 1   | 1   |

- A trap into M-mode writes fields GVA, MPIE, and MIE in mstatus/mstatush.

- Upon trap return, MPV is ignored when MPP=3.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When a trap is taken from U-mode into M-mode, the MPV and MPP fields in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable, are set to 0 and 0, respectively.

RULE 2: When a trap is taken from HS-mode into M-mode, the MPV and MPP fields in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable, are set to 0 and 1, respectively.

RULE 3: When a trap is taken from M-mode into M-mode, the MPV and MPP fields in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable, are set to 0 and 3, respectively.

RULE 4: When a trap is taken from VU-mode into M-mode, the MPV and MPP fields in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable, are set to 1 and 0, respectively.

RULE 5: When a trap is taken from VS-mode into M-mode, the MPV and MPP fields in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable, are set to 1 and 1, respectively.

RULE 6: A trap taken into M-mode writes the GVA field in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable.

RULE 7: A trap taken into M-mode writes the MPIE field in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable.

RULE 8: A trap taken into M-mode writes the MIE field in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable.

RULE 9: Upon trap return, the MPV field in mstatus or mstatush, as applicable, is ignored when the MPP field in mstatus is 3.

The following description:

- The MRET instruction is used to return from a trap taken into M-mode.

- MRET first determines what the new privilege mode will be according to the values of MPP and MPV in mstatus or mstatush.

- MRET then sets the following fields in mstatus/mstatush:

  - MPP=0.

=========

RULE 5: MRET sets MPIE to 1 in mstatus or mstatush.

RULE 6: After updating mstatus or mstatush, MRET sets the privilege mode to the mode determined from the earlier values of MPP and MPV in mstatus or mstatush.

The following description:

- A trap may need to be delivered to the same or to a higher privilege mode upon completion of JALR, C.JALR, or C.JR, but before the instruction at the target of the indirect call/jump was decoded, due to:
  - asynchronous interrupts; or
  - synchronous exceptions with priority higher than that of a software-check exception with xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".

  In such cases, the ELP prior to the trap, the previous ELP, must be preserved by the trap delivery such that it can be restored on a return from the trap.

- To store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M-mode, an MPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR.

- To store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to S/HS-mode, an SPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR.

- The SPELP bit in mstatus can be accessed through the sstatus CSR.

- When a trap is taken into privilege mode x, the xPELP is set to ELP, and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED. For trap delivery to M-mode, xPELP is MPELP in mstatus. For trap delivery to S/HS-mode, xPELP is SPELP in mstatus.

- An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode, respectively.

- When executing an xRET instruction, if the new privilege mode is y and yLPE is 1, ELP is set to the value of xPELP.

- When executing an xRET instruction, if the new privilege mode is y and yLPE is not 1, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

- When executing an xRET instruction, xPELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

- The trap handler in privilege mode x must save the xPELP bit before performing an indirect call/jump if xLPE=1.

- If privilege mode x can respond to interrupts and xLPE=1, the trap handler should also save the xPELP bit before enabling interrupts.

- The trap handler in privilege mode x must restore the saved xPELP bit before executing the xRET instruction to return from a trap.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus CSR provides the MPELP bit to store the previous ELP state when a trap is delivered to M-mode.

RULE 2: The mstatus CSR provides the SPELP bit to store the previous ELP state when a trap is delivered to S/HS-mode.

RULE 3: The SPELP bit in mstatus can be accessed through the sstatus CSR.

RULE 4: If an asynchronous interrupt causes a trap to be delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode after completion of JALR, C.JALR, or C.JR but before decoding the instruction at the indirect call or jump target, trap delivery must preserve the previous ELP in mstatus.MPELP for M-mode or mstatus.SPELP for S/HS-mode so that ELP can be restored on trap return.

RULE 5: If a synchronous exception with priority higher than a software-check exception having xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)" causes a trap to be delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode after completion of JALR, C.JALR, or C.JR but before decoding the instruction at the indirect call or jump target, trap delivery must preserve the previous ELP in mstatus.MPELP for M-mode or mstatus.SPELP for S/HS-mode so that ELP can be restored on trap return.

RULE 6: When a trap is taken into M-mode, mstatus.MPELP is set to the value of ELP.

RULE 7: When a trap is taken into S/HS-mode, mstatus.SPELP is set to the value of ELP.

RULE 8: When a trap is taken into M-mode, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED as part of the trap operation that stores the previous ELP in mstatus.MPELP.

RULE 9: When a trap is taken into S/HS-mode, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED as part of the trap operation that stores the previous ELP in mstatus.SPELP.

RULE 10: When an MRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode is y with yLPE equal to 1, ELP is set to the value of mstatus.MPELP.

RULE 11: When an SRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode is y with yLPE equal to 1, ELP is set to the value of mstatus.SPELP.

RULE 12: When an MRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode is y with yLPE not equal to 1, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED regardless of the value of mstatus.MPELP.

RULE 13: When an SRET instruction executes and the new privilege mode is y with yLPE not equal to 1, ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED regardless of the value of mstatus.SPELP.

RULE 14: When an MRET instruction executes, mstatus.MPELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

RULE 15: When an SRET instruction executes, mstatus.SPELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

RULE 16: An M-mode trap handler must save mstatus.MPELP before performing an indirect call or jump if MLPE is 1.

RULE 17: An S/HS-mode trap handler must save mstatus.SPELP before performing an indirect call or jump if SLPE is 1.

RULE 18: If M-mode can respond to interrupts and MLPE is 1, an M-mode trap handler should save mstatus.MPELP before enabling interrupts.

RULE 19: If S/HS-mode can respond to interrupts and SLPE is 1, an S/HS-mode trap handler should save mstatus.SPELP before enabling interrupts.

RULE 20: An M-mode trap handler must restore the saved mstatus.MPELP bit before executing MRET to return from a trap.

RULE 21: An S/HS-mode trap handler must restore the saved mstatus.SPELP bit before executing SRET to return from a trap.
======================================================= END - 48 =======================================================

The following description:

- The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit in the mstatus register is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations.

- When TVM=1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=0, attempts to read or write the satp CSR are permitted in S-mode.

- TVM is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

- Trapping satp accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables.

RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The WARL TVM bit in the mstatus register supports intercepting S-mode accesses to the satp CSR as supervisor virtual-memory management operations.

RULE 2: When TVM=1, an attempt to read the satp CSR while executing in S-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 3: When TVM=1, an attempt to write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 4: When TVM=0, an attempt to read the satp CSR while executing in S-mode is permitted.

RULE 5: When TVM=0, an attempt to write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode is permitted.

RULE 6: When S-mode is not supported, the mstatus TVM bit that controls S-mode access to the satp CSR is read-only 0.

RULE 7: Trapping satp accesses together with trapping the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables.

The following description:

- If S-mode is not supported, or if satp.MODE is read-only zero (always Bare), the implementation may make menvcfg.FIOM read-only zero.

- The menvcfg.PBMTE bit controls whether the Svpbmt extension is available for use in S-mode address translation, i.e., for page tables pointed to by satp.

RULE 2: When menvcfg.PBMTE equals 1, the Svpbmt extension is available for S-mode address translation using page tables pointed to by satp.

RULE 3: When menvcfg.PBMTE equals 0, the implementation behaves as though the Svpbmt extension were not implemented for S-mode address translation using page tables pointed to by satp.

RULE 4: If the Svpbmt extension is not implemented, menvcfg.PBMTE is read-only zero for S-mode address translation using page tables pointed to by satp.

The following description:

1. The satp CSR is an SXLEN-bit read/write register that controls supervisor-mode address translation and protection.

2. The satp register holds:
   - The physical page number (PPN) of the root page table, i.e., its supervisor physical address divided by 4 KiB.
   - An address space identifier (ASID), which facilitates address-translation fences on a per-address-space basis.
   - The MODE field, which selects the current address-translation scheme.

3. Figure 63. Supervisor address translation and protection (satp) register when SXLEN=32.

| Field | Bit range | Width | Type |
|-------|-----------|-------|------|
| MODE  | 31        | 1     | WARL |
| ASID  | 30:22     | 9     | WARL |
| PPN   | 21:0      | 22    | WARL |

4. Storing a PPN in satp, rather than a physical address, supports a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32.

5. The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers.

6. Some platform standards might place constraints on the values satp.PPN may assume, e.g., by requiring that all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory be representable.

7. Figure 64. Supervisor address translation and protection (satp) register when SXLEN=64, for MODE values Bare, Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.

| Field | Bit range | Width | Type |
|-------|-----------|-------|------|
| MODE  | 63:60     | 4     | WARL |
| ASID  | 59:44     | 16    | WARL |
| PPN   | 43:0      | 44    | WARL |

8. The ASID and the page table base address are stored in the same CSR to allow the pair to be changed atomically on a context switch. Swapping them non-atomically could pollute the old virtual address space with new translations, or vice-versa. This approach also slightly reduces the cost of a context switch.

9. When MODE=Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses, and there is no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme described in Section 3.7.

10. To select MODE=Bare, software must write zero to the remaining fields of satp:
    - Bits 30:0 when SXLEN=32.
    - Bits 59:0 when SXLEN=64.

11. Attempting to select MODE=Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining fields has:
    - An UNSPECIFIED effect on the value that the remaining fields assume.
    - An UNSPECIFIED effect on address translation and protection behavior.

12. When SXLEN=32, the satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use.

13. When SXLEN=32, the satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare and ASID[8:7] not equal to 3 are reserved for future standard use.

14. When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE=Bare are reserved for future standard use.

15. Version 1.11 of this standard stated that the remaining fields in satp had no effect when MODE=Bare. Making these fields reserved facilitates future definition of additional translation and protection modes, particularly in RV32, for which all patterns of the existing MODE field have already been allocated.

16. When SXLEN=32, the only valid setting for MODE other than Bare is Sv32, a paged virtual-memory scheme described in Section 12.3.

17. When SXLEN=64, three paged virtual-memory schemes are defined:
    - Sv39, described in Section 12.4.
    - Sv48, described in Section 12.5.
    - Sv57, described in Section 12.6.

18. When SXLEN=64, one additional scheme, Sv64, will be defined in a later version of this specification.

19. When SXLEN=64, the remaining MODE settings are reserved for future use and may define different interpretations of the other fields in satp.

20. Table 40. Encoding of satp MODE field.

| SXLEN | Value | Name | Description |
|-------|-------|------|-------------|
| 32 | 0 | Bare | No translation or protection. |
| 32 | 1 | Sv32 | Page-based 32-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.3). |
| 64 | 0 | Bare | No translation or protection. |
| 64 | 1-7 | - | Reserved for standard use. |
| 64 | 8 | Sv39 | Page-based 39-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.4). |
| 64 | 9 | Sv48 | Page-based 48-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.5). |
| 64 | 10 | Sv57 | Page-based 57-bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.6). |
| 64 | 11 | Sv64 | Reserved for page-based 64-bit virtual addressing. |
| 64 | 12-13 | - | Reserved for standard use. |
| 64 | 14-15 | - | Designated for custom use. |

21. Implementations are not required to support all MODE settings.

22. If satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect; no fields in satp are modified.

23. The number of ASID bits is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero.

24. The number of implemented ASID bits is termed ASIDLEN.

25. ASIDLEN may be determined by writing one to every bit position in the ASID field, then reading back the value in satp to see which bit positions in the ASID field hold a one.

26. The least-significant bits of ASID are implemented first: if ASIDLEN > 0, ASID[ASIDLEN-1:0] is writable.

27. The maximal value of ASIDLEN, termed ASIDMAX, is:
    - 9 for Sv32.
    - 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.

28. The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode.

29. Executions of the address-translation algorithm may only begin using a given value of satp when satp is active.

30. Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.

31. The SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to the address-translation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads to those structures by a hart.

32. Writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page-table updates and subsequent address translations.

33. Writing satp does not imply any invalidation of address-translation caches.

34. If the new address space's page tables have been modified, or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.

35. Not imposing upon implementations to flush address-translation caches upon satp writes reduces the cost of context switches, provided a sufficiently large ASID space.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The satp CSR is an SXLEN-bit read/write register.

RULE 2: The satp register controls supervisor-mode address translation and protection.

RULE 3: The satp.PPN field holds the physical page number of the root page table, equal to its supervisor physical address divided by 4 KiB.

RULE 4: The satp.ASID field holds an address space identifier that facilitates address-translation fences on a per-address-space basis.

RULE 5: The satp.MODE field selects the current address-translation scheme.

RULE 6: When SXLEN=32, satp.MODE occupies bit 31, has a width of 1 bit, and is WARL.

RULE 7: When SXLEN=32, satp.ASID occupies bits 30:22, has a width of 9 bits, and is WARL.

RULE 8: When SXLEN=32, satp.PPN occupies bits 21:0, has a width of 22 bits, and is WARL.

RULE 9: Storing a physical page number rather than a physical address in satp supports an RV32 physical address space larger than 4 GiB.

RULE 10: The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding every physical page number.

RULE 11: Some platform standards might constrain the values that satp.PPN may assume, such as by requiring all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory to be representable.

RULE 12: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE occupies bits 63:60, has a width of 4 bits, and is WARL.

RULE 13: When SXLEN=64, satp.ASID occupies bits 59:44, has a width of 16 bits, and is WARL.

RULE 14: When SXLEN=64, satp.PPN occupies bits 43:0, has a width of 44 bits, and is WARL.

RULE 15: The satp register stores the ASID and page-table base address together so that software can change the pair atomically during a context switch.

RULE 16: Changing the satp ASID and page-table base address non-atomically could pollute the old virtual address space with new translations or the new virtual address space with old translations.

RULE 17: Storing the ASID and page-table base address together in satp slightly reduces the cost of a context switch.

RULE 18: When satp.MODE=Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses.

RULE 19: When satp.MODE=Bare, satp provides no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme.

RULE 20: When SXLEN=32, software must write zero to satp bits 30:0 to select satp.MODE=Bare.

RULE 21: When SXLEN=64, software must write zero to satp bits 59:0 to select satp.MODE=Bare.

RULE 22: Attempting to select satp.MODE=Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining satp fields has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the values assumed by those fields.

RULE 23: Attempting to select satp.MODE=Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining satp fields has an UNSPECIFIED effect on address-translation and protection behavior.

RULE 24: When SXLEN=32, satp encodings with satp.MODE=Bare and satp.ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use.

RULE 25: When SXLEN=32, satp encodings with satp.MODE=Bare and satp.ASID[8:7] not equal to 3 are reserved for future standard use.

RULE 26: When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings with satp.MODE=Bare are reserved for future standard use.

RULE 27: Version 1.11 of the standard stated that satp fields other than MODE had no effect when satp.MODE=Bare.

RULE 28: Reserving satp fields other than MODE when satp.MODE=Bare facilitates future definitions of additional address-translation and protection modes.

RULE 29: When SXLEN=32, every pattern of the existing satp.MODE field has already been allocated.

RULE 30: When SXLEN=32, Sv32 is the only valid satp.MODE setting other than Bare.

RULE 31: When SXLEN=32, satp.MODE value 0 is named Bare and specifies no address translation or protection.

RULE 32: When SXLEN=32, satp.MODE value 1 selects Sv32 page-based 32-bit virtual addressing.

RULE 33: When SXLEN=64, satp defines the three paged virtual-memory schemes Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.

RULE 34: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE value 0 is named Bare and specifies no address translation or protection.

RULE 35: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE values 1 through 7 are reserved for standard use.

RULE 36: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE value 8 selects Sv39 page-based 39-bit virtual addressing.

RULE 37: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE value 9 selects Sv48 page-based 48-bit virtual addressing.

RULE 38: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE value 10 selects Sv57 page-based 57-bit virtual addressing.

RULE 39: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE value 11 is named Sv64 and is reserved for page-based 64-bit virtual addressing.

RULE 40: When SXLEN=64, the Sv64 address-translation scheme represented by satp.MODE will be defined in a later version of the specification.

RULE 41: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE values 12 and 13 are reserved for standard use.

RULE 42: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE values 14 and 15 are designated for custom use.

RULE 43: When SXLEN=64, satp.MODE settings other than Bare, Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57 are reserved for future use and may define different interpretations of the other satp fields.

RULE 44: An implementation is not required to support every satp.MODE setting.

RULE 45: If satp is written with an unsupported satp.MODE value, the entire write has no effect and no satp field is modified.

RULE 46: The number of implemented bits in satp.ASID is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero.

RULE 47: ASIDLEN is the number of implemented bits in satp.ASID.

RULE 48: ASIDLEN may be determined by writing one to every bit position in satp.ASID and then reading satp to determine which satp.ASID bit positions hold one.

RULE 49: The least-significant bits of satp.ASID are implemented first.

RULE 50: If ASIDLEN is greater than zero, satp.ASID[ASIDLEN-1:0] is writable.

RULE 51: ASIDMAX is the maximum value of ASIDLEN for satp.

RULE 52: When satp.MODE=Sv32, ASIDMAX is 9.

RULE 53: When satp.MODE is Sv39, Sv48, or Sv57, ASIDMAX is 16.

RULE 54: The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S-mode or U-mode.

RULE 55: An execution of the address-translation algorithm may begin using a given satp value only while satp is active.

RULE 56: Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate after satp becomes inactive unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.

RULE 57: For address translation controlled by satp, the SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to address-translation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads of those structures by a hart.

RULE 58: Writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page-table updates and subsequent address translations.

RULE 59: Writing satp does not imply any invalidation of address-translation caches.

RULE 60: If the page tables of the new address space have been modified, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.

RULE 61: If an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.

RULE 62: Not requiring address-translation caches to be flushed when satp is written reduces context-switch cost when the ASID space is sufficiently large.
======================================================= END - 56 =======================================================

The following description:

1. This property applies even if the virtual-address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE. For a given virtual address and ASID, any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, even if that translation used a virtual address of a different width. Similarly, for a given virtual address, any global translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, regardless of both ASID and virtual-address width.

2. Implementations must only perform implicit reads of the translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of the satp register or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation data structure entry.

3. Changing satp.MODE from Bare to other modes and vice versa takes effect immediately, without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.

4. Changes to satp.ASID take effect immediately.

5. When software recycles an ASID, i.e., reassociates it with a different page table, it should first change satp to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID, then execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID.

6. Alternatively, when software recycles an ASID, software can execute the same SFENCE.VMA instruction while a different ASID is loaded into satp, provided the next time satp is loaded with the recycled ASID, it is simultaneously loaded with the new page table.

7. If the implementation does not provide ASIDs, or software chooses to always use ASID 0, then after every satp write, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0.

8. If the implementation does not provide ASIDs, or software chooses to always use ASID 0, then, in the common case that no global translations have been modified, rs2 should be set to a register other than x0 but which contains the value zero, so that global translations are not flushed.

9. If a hart employs an address-translation cache, that cache must appear to be private to that hart. In particular, the meaning of an ASID is local to a hart; software may choose to use the same ASID to refer to different address spaces on different harts.

10. For implementations that make satp.MODE read-only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal-instruction exception.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: For a given virtual address and ASID, any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used even if that translation used a virtual-address width different from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.

RULE 2: For a given virtual address, any global translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used regardless of its ASID and regardless of whether its virtual-address width differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.

RULE 3: An implementation must perform implicit reads only from translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of satp or by a subsequent valid translation data structure entry with V=1.

RULE 4: Changing satp.MODE from Bare to any other mode takes effect immediately without requiring execution of an SFENCE.VMA instruction.

RULE 5: Changing satp.MODE from any non-Bare mode to Bare takes effect immediately without requiring execution of an SFENCE.VMA instruction.

RULE 6: Changes to satp.ASID take effect immediately.

RULE 7: When software recycles an ASID by reassociating it with a different page table, software should first change satp to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID and then execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID.

RULE 8: When software recycles an ASID by reassociating it with a different page table, software can execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID while a different ASID is loaded in satp, provided that the next write loading the recycled ASID into satp simultaneously changes satp to point to the new page table.

RULE 9: If an implementation does not provide ASIDs or software always uses ASID 0, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1=x0 after every write to satp.

RULE 10: If an implementation does not provide ASIDs or software always uses ASID 0, then in the common case where no global translations have been modified, the SFENCE.VMA executed after a satp write should use rs2 set to a register other than x0 that contains zero so that global translations are not flushed.

RULE 11: If a hart employs an address-translation cache for address translation controlled by satp, that cache must appear to be private to that hart.

RULE 12: The meaning of an ASID stored in satp.ASID is local to a hart.

RULE 13: Software may choose to use the same satp.ASID value to refer to different address spaces on different harts.

RULE 14: If satp.MODE is read-only zero so that satp is always in Bare mode, an attempt to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal-instruction exception.

The following description:

- When Sv32 is written to the MODE field in the satp register, the supervisor operates in a 32-bit paged virtual-memory system.

The following description:

1. When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of the satp register, supervisor virtual addresses are translated into supervisor physical addresses via a two-level page table. The 20-bit virtual page number (VPN) is translated into a 22-bit physical page number (PPN), while the 12-bit page offset is untranslated.

2. When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of the satp register, the resulting supervisor-level physical addresses are checked using any physical memory protection structures before being directly converted to machine-level physical addresses. If necessary, supervisor-level physical addresses are zero-extended to the number of physical address bits found in the implementation.

3. For an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address, when the value of satp.MODE is Sv32, a 34-bit physical address is produced directly, and therefore no zero extension is needed.

4. For an RV32 system supporting 34 bits of physical address, when the value of satp.MODE is Bare, the 32-bit virtual address is translated unmodified into a 32-bit physical address, and that physical address is then zero-extended into a 34-bit machine-level physical address.

5. For Sv32 page tables, the physical page number of the root page table is stored in the satp register. An Sv32 page table is exactly the size of a page and must always be aligned to a page boundary.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of satp, supervisor virtual addresses are translated into supervisor physical addresses through a two-level page table.

RULE 2: When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of satp, the 20-bit virtual page number is translated into a 22-bit physical page number.

RULE 3: When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of satp, the 12-bit page offset remains untranslated.

RULE 4: When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of satp, the resulting supervisor-level physical addresses are checked using any physical memory protection structures before being directly converted to machine-level physical addresses.

RULE 5: When Sv32 virtual memory mode is selected in the MODE field of satp, supervisor-level physical addresses are zero-extended to the implementation's number of physical address bits if necessary.

RULE 6: In an RV32 system supporting 34 physical address bits, when the MODE field of satp is Sv32, address translation directly produces a 34-bit physical address.

RULE 7: In an RV32 system supporting 34 physical address bits, when the MODE field of satp is Sv32, the produced physical address does not require zero extension.

RULE 8: In an RV32 system supporting 34 physical address bits, when the MODE field of satp is Bare, the 32-bit virtual address is translated unmodified into a 32-bit physical address.

RULE 9: In an RV32 system supporting 34 physical address bits, when the MODE field of satp is Bare, the resulting 32-bit physical address is zero-extended into a 34-bit machine-level physical address.

RULE 10: For Sv32 page tables, satp stores the physical page number of the root page table.

RULE 11: Every Sv32 page table in the page-table hierarchy rooted at the physical page number stored in satp is exactly the size of one page.

RULE 12: Every Sv32 page table in the page-table hierarchy rooted at the physical page number stored in satp must always be aligned to a page boundary.

The following description:

1. During virtual-address translation, let `a` be `satp.ppn x PAGESIZE`, and let `i = LEVELS-1`. For Sv32, `PAGESIZE = 2^12` and `LEVELS = 2`. The `satp` register must be active, i.e., the effective privilege mode must be S-mode or U-mode.

2. Implementations may execute the address-translation algorithm speculatively at any time, for any virtual address, as long as `satp` is active. Such speculative executions have the effect of pre-populating the address-translation cache.

3. Speculative executions of the address-translation algorithm behave as non-speculative executions of the algorithm do, except that they must not set the dirty bit for a PTE, they must not trigger an exception, and they must not create address-translation cache entries if those entries would have been invalidated by any `SFENCE.VMA` instruction executed by the hart since the speculative execution of the algorithm began.

4. There is no explicit prohibition against placing page tables in non-idempotent memory. Since the algorithm may only touch page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in `satp`, the range of addresses that an implementation's page-table walker will touch is fully under supervisor control.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: During virtual-address translation, a is defined as satp.ppn * PAGESIZE.

RULE 2: During virtual-address translation using satp, i is defined as LEVELS - 1.

RULE 3: For Sv32 virtual-address translation using satp, PAGESIZE is 2^12.

RULE 4: For Sv32 virtual-address translation using satp, LEVELS is 2.

RULE 5: During virtual-address translation, satp MUST be active, meaning that the effective privilege mode MUST be S-mode or U-mode.

RULE 6: A speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm while satp is active has the effect of pre-populating the address-translation cache.

RULE 7: Except for PTE dirty-bit setting, exception triggering, and the restricted creation of address-translation cache entries, a speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm while satp is active behaves as a non-speculative execution of the algorithm.

RULE 8: A speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm while satp is active MUST NOT set the dirty bit for a PTE.

RULE 9: A speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm while satp is active MUST NOT trigger an exception.

RULE 10: A speculative execution of the address-translation algorithm while satp is active MUST NOT create an address-translation cache entry if that entry would have been invalidated by any SFENCE.VMA instruction executed by the hart since the speculative execution began.

RULE 11: There is no explicit prohibition against placing page tables used for address translation under satp in non-idempotent memory.

RULE 12: The address-translation algorithm may touch only page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in satp.

RULE 13: The range of addresses that an implementation's page-table walker will touch under satp is fully under supervisor control because the algorithm may touch only page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in satp.

The following description:

- The vsatp register is VS-mode's version of supervisor register satp.

- When V=1, vsatp substitutes for the usual satp, so instructions that normally read or modify satp actually access vsatp instead.

- When V=0, a write to vsatp with an unsupported MODE value is either ignored as it is for satp, or the fields of vsatp are treated as WARL in the normal way.

- When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value is ignored and no write to vsatp is effected.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The vsatp register is the VS-mode version of the supervisor-level satp register.

RULE 2: When V=1, vsatp substitutes for satp, so an instruction that normally reads satp accesses vsatp instead.

RULE 3: When V=1, vsatp substitutes for satp, so an instruction that normally modifies satp accesses vsatp instead.

RULE 4: When V=0, a write to vsatp with an unsupported MODE value is either ignored in the same manner as such a write to satp, or the fields of vsatp are treated as WARL in the normal way.

RULE 5: When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value is ignored.

RULE 6: When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value does not effect a write to vsatp.

The following description:

- When applied to a physical address, including guest-physical addresses (i.e., all cases except when the active satp register's MODE field != Bare), the ignore transformation replaces the upper PMLEN bits with 0:

```text
transformed_effective_address =
{{PMLEN{0}}, effective_address[XLEN-PMLEN-1:0]}
```

- This includes both the case of running in M-mode and running in other privilege modes with Bare address translation mode.

- For virtual addresses, the ignore transformation replaces the upper PMLEN bits with the sign extension of the PMLEN+1st bit:

```text
transformed_effective_address =
{{PMLEN{effective_address[XLEN-PMLEN-1]}},
 effective_address[XLEN-PMLEN-1:0]}
```
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When the ignore transformation is applied to a physical address, including a guest-physical address, while running in M-mode or while running in another privilege mode with the active satp register's MODE field set to Bare, it replaces the upper PMLEN bits with 0 and produces transformed_effective_address = {{PMLEN{0}}, effective_address[XLEN-PMLEN-1:0]}.

RULE 2: When the active satp register's MODE field is not Bare and the ignore transformation is applied to a virtual address, it replaces the upper PMLEN bits with the sign extension of the PMLEN+1st bit and produces transformed_effective_address = {{PMLEN{effective_address[XLEN-PMLEN-1]}}, effective_address[XLEN-PMLEN-1:0]}.

The following description:

- The counter-enable (`scounteren`) CSR is a 32-bit register that controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.

- When the CY bit in `scounteren` is clear, attempts to read the `cycle` register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception. When the CY bit is set, access to the `cycle` register is permitted.

- When the TM bit in `scounteren` is clear, attempts to read the `time` register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception. When the TM bit is set, access to the `time` register is permitted.

- When the IR bit in `scounteren` is clear, attempts to read the `instret` register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception. When the IR bit is set, access to the `instret` register is permitted.

- When the HPMn bit in `scounteren` is clear, attempts to read the corresponding `hpmcountern` register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception. When the HPMn bit is set, access to the corresponding `hpmcountern` register is permitted.

- `scounteren` must be implemented.

- Any of the bits in `scounteren` may be read-only zero, indicating reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U-mode. Hence, the bits are effectively WARL fields.

- The setting of a bit in `mcounteren` does not affect whether the corresponding bit in `scounteren` is writable.

- U-mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in `scounteren` and `mcounteren` are both set.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The scounteren CSR is a 32-bit register.

RULE 2: The scounteren CSR controls the availability of hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode.

RULE 3: When the CY bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the cycle register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 4: When the CY bit in scounteren is set, U-mode access to the cycle register is permitted.

RULE 5: When the TM bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the time register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 6: When the TM bit in scounteren is set, U-mode access to the time register is permitted.

RULE 7: When the IR bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the instret register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 8: When the IR bit in scounteren is set, U-mode access to the instret register is permitted.

RULE 9: When an HPMn bit in scounteren is clear, an attempt to read the corresponding hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 10: When an HPMn bit in scounteren is set, U-mode access to the corresponding hpmcountern register is permitted.

RULE 11: The scounteren CSR MUST be implemented.

RULE 12: Any bit in scounteren may be read-only zero.

RULE 13: A read-only-zero bit in scounteren indicates that reading the corresponding counter while executing in U-mode will cause an exception.

RULE 14: The bits in scounteren are effectively WARL fields.

RULE 15: Setting a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.

RULE 16: U-mode may access a counter only if the corresponding bits in scounteren and mcounteren are both set.

The following description:

- When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if the attempted instruction is HS-qualified but is prevented from executing when V=1 because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren. An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode, for some values of the instruction's register operands, assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.

- In VU-mode, attempts to access a non-high-half counter CSR raise a virtual-instruction exception when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.

- In VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access a high-half counter CSR raise a virtual-instruction exception when the corresponding bit in either hcounteren or scounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, a virtual-instruction exception with code 22 is normally raised instead of an illegal-instruction exception if an attempted instruction is expressly disabled by scounteren and would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of its register operands assuming the TSR and TVM fields of mstatus are both zero.

RULE 2: In VU-mode, an attempt to access a non-high-half counter CSR raises a virtual-instruction exception when the corresponding bit in either scounteren or hcounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.

RULE 3: In VU-mode when XLEN=32, an attempt to access a high-half counter CSR raises a virtual-instruction exception when the corresponding bit in either scounteren or hcounteren is 0 and the same bit in mcounteren is 1.
